in most applications, particularly later when we use this to block-encode the molecular hamiltonian, the total number of bit-strings  $q$  is significantly larger than  $j$ . thus the cost of computation in lemma 7 is dominated by  $\mathrm{d}_{q,b,\lambda} + \mathcal{o}(n_q + \mathrm{d}_{j,n_q,n})$ .

# c. double-factorized hamiltonian

the electronic hamiltonian in first-quantization is

$$
h _ {\text {f i r s t}} = \left(- \sum_ {n \in \text {e l e c t r o n s}} \frac {\nabla_ {n} ^ {2}}{2} - \sum_ {m \in \text {n u c l e i}} \frac {z _ {m}}{\left| x _ {n} - r _ {m} \right|}\right) + \left(\sum_ {n _ {1}, n _ {2} \in \text {e l e c t r o n s}} \frac {1}{\left| x _ {n _ {1}} - x _ {n _ {2}} \right|}\right), \tag {44}
$$

where  $\nabla_{n}^{2}$  is the laplace operator on the  $n^{\mathrm{th}}$  electron,  $z_{m}$  is the nuclear charge, and  $r_m$  is the nucleus coordinate. by choosing a basis of orbitals  $\psi_i(x)$ , this implies the second-quantized representation

$$
h = \sum_ {i j, \sigma} h _ {i j} a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(j, \sigma)} + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i j k l, \sigma \rho} h _ {i j k l} a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(k, \rho)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(l, \rho)} a _ {(j, \sigma)}, \tag {45}
$$

$$
h _ {i j} = \int \psi_ {i} ^ {*} (x _ {1}) \left(- \frac {\nabla^ {2}}{2} - \sum_ {m} \frac {z _ {m}}{| x _ {1} - r _ {m} |}\right) \psi_ {j} (x _ {1}) \mathrm {d} ^ {3} x _ {1},
$$

$$
h _ {i j l k} = \int \psi_ {i} ^ {*} (x _ {1}) \psi_ {j} (x _ {1}) \left(\frac {1}{| x _ {1} - x _ {2} |}\right) \psi_ {k} ^ {*} (x _ {2}) \psi_ {l} (x _ {2}) \mathrm {d} ^ {3} x _ {1} \mathrm {d} ^ {3} x _ {2}.
$$

this is equal to the single-factorized  $h_{\mathrm{cd}}$  and double-factorized  $h_{\mathrm{df}}$  hamiltonians

$$
h _ {\mathrm {c d}} \doteq \sum_ {i j, \sigma} \tilde {h} _ {i j} a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(j, \sigma)} + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {r \in [ r ]} \left(\sum_ {i j, \sigma} l _ {i j} ^ {(r)} a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(j, \sigma)}\right) ^ {2}, \quad \tilde {h} _ {i j} \doteq h _ {i j} - \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {l} h _ {i l l j}, \tag {46}
$$

$$
h _ {\mathrm {d f}} = \sum_ {i j, \sigma} \tilde {h} _ {i j} a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(j, \sigma)} + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {r \in [ r ]} \left(\sum_ {i j, \sigma} \sum_ {m \in [ m ^ {(r)} ]} \lambda_ {m} ^ {(r)} \vec {r} _ {m, i} ^ {(r)} \cdot \vec {r} _ {m, j} ^ {(r) \top} a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(j, \sigma)}\right) ^ {2},
$$

where the  $\lambda_{m}^{(r)}$  are eigenvalues of  $l^{(r)}$ .

the dominant cost in qubitizing a hamiltonian is the synthesis of a unitary quantum circuit  $\mathcal{b}[h / \alpha]$  with the property

$$
\mathcal {b} [ h / \alpha ] = \left( \begin{array}{c c} h / \alpha & \dots \\ \vdots & \ddots \end{array} \right). \tag {47}
$$

this implies that  $\mathcal{b}[h / \alpha] |0\rangle_a |\psi\rangle_s = |0\rangle_a \frac{h}{\alpha} |\psi\rangle_s + |0\psi^\perp\rangle_{as}$ , where the unnormalized residual state  $|0\psi^\perp\rangle_{as}$  has no support on the ancilla state  $|0\rangle_a$ . as  $h$  is embedded in a contiguous block of the unitary, we say that  $b[h / \alpha]$  'block-encodes' the hamiltonian  $h$ .

the block-encoding framework supports the addition and multiplication of encoded matrices. for instance, one may add block-encoded hamiltonians  $h = \sum_{j}h_{j}$  with a new normalizing constant such as  $\alpha = \sum_{j}\alpha_{1}$ , using the following quantum circuit

$$
\mathcal {b} [ h / \alpha ] = \left(\sum_ {j} \langle j | _ {a} \otimes \mathcal {i} _ {s} \sqrt {\frac {\alpha_ {j}}{\alpha}}\right) \left(\sum_ {j} | j \rangle \langle j | _ {a} \otimes \mathcal {b} [ h _ {j} / \alpha_ {j} ]\right) \left(\sum_ {j} \sqrt {\frac {\alpha_ {j}}{\alpha}} | j \rangle_ {a} \otimes \mathcal {i} _ {s}\right). \tag {48}
$$

as mentioned in eq. (14), block-encoded hamiltonians may also be multiplied to obtain  $\mathcal{b}[h_2h_1 / \alpha_2\alpha_1]$ . in general, these addition and multiplication operations increase the ancilla register size in a straightforward manner. using these ingredients, one may block-encode matrices specified by a variety of common input models, such as sparse matrix oracle, linear-combination-unitaries, or other quantum data structures.

in qubitizing  $h_{\mathrm{df}}$ , we find it more natural to work in the majorana representation of the fermion operators

$$
\gamma_ {p, 0} = a _ {p} + a _ {p} ^ {\dagger}, \quad \gamma_ {p, 1} = - i \left(a _ {p} - a _ {p} ^ {\dagger}\right), \quad \left\{\gamma_ {p, x}, \gamma_ {q, y} \right\} = 2 \delta_ {p q} \delta_ {x y} \mathcal {i}. \tag {49}
$$

thus  $a_{p} = (\gamma_{p,0} + i\gamma_{p,1}) / 2$  and  $a_{q} = (\gamma_{p,0} - i\gamma_{p,1}) / 2$ , where  $p \doteq (i,\sigma)$  is a combined orbital and spin index. some useful identities are

$$
a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(j, \sigma)} + a _ {(j, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(i, \sigma)} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \mathcal {i} + i (\gamma_ {i, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {i, \sigma , 1}), & i = j, \\ \frac {i}{2} (\gamma_ {i, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {j, \sigma , 1} + \gamma_ {j, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {i, \sigma , 1}), & i \neq j, \end{array} \right. \tag {50}
$$

which implies the majorana representation for one-electron hamiltonian

$$
\sum_ {i j, \sigma} l _ {i j} a _ {(i, \sigma)} ^ {\dagger} a _ {(j, \sigma)} = \sum_ {i} l _ {i i} \mathcal {i} + \operatorname {o n e} _ {l}, \quad \operatorname {o n e} _ {l} \doteq \frac {i}{2} \sum_ {i j} \sum_ {\sigma} l _ {i j} \gamma_ {i, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {j, \sigma , 1} \tag {51}
$$

that separates into a trivial identity component, and a non-trivial one-electron component  $\mathrm{one}_l$ . as  $l$  is a symmetric matrix, it has the eigendecomposition  $l = \sum_{k} \lambda_k \vec{r}_k \cdot \vec{r}_k^\top$ . thus we may diagonalize the one-electron hamiltonian to obtain

$$
\operatorname {o n e} _ {l} = \frac {i}{2} \sum_ {k} \lambda_ {k} \sum_ {\sigma} \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 1}, \quad \gamma_ {\vec {u}, \sigma , x} \doteq \sum_ {j} u _ {j} \gamma_ {j, \sigma , x}. \tag {52}
$$

one should verify that  $\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}^2 = \mathcal{i}$ , which follows from eq. (49) and the unit-length normalization eq. (52) of  $\vec{u}$ .

thus the spectral norm  $\| \mathrm{one}_l\| = \| l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}$  is seen to be the one-norm of the eigenvalues of  $l$ . substituting the majorana representation into the doubly-factorized hamiltonian results in

$$
h _ {\mathrm {d f}} = \left(\sum_ {i} h _ {i i} - \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i l} h _ {i l l i} + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i l} h _ {l l i i}\right) \mathcal {i} + \operatorname {o n e} _ {l ^ {(- 1)}} + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {r} \operatorname {o n e} _ {l ^ {(r)}} ^ {2}, \tag {53}
$$

$$
l _ {i j} ^ {(- 1)} \doteq h _ {i j} - \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {l} h _ {i l l j} + \sum_ {l} h _ {l l i j}.
$$

our strategy for block-encoding eq. (53) is to build it up from block-encodings of its component pieces. let us further collect terms as follows.

$$
h _ {\mathrm {d f}} = \left(\sum_ {i} h _ {i i} - \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i l} h _ {i l l i} + \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {i l} h _ {l l i i} + \frac {1}{4} \sum_ {r} \| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2}\right) \mathcal {i} + \operatorname {o n e} _ {l ^ {(- 1)}} + \operatorname {t w o} _ {h},
$$

$$
\mathrm {t w o} _ {h} \doteq \frac {1}{4} \sum_ {r} \| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2} t _ {2} \left[ \frac {\operatorname {o n e} _ {l ^ {(r)}}}{\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}}} \right], \tag {54}
$$

where  $t_{2}(x) = 2x^{2} - 1$  is a chebyshev polynomial of the first kind. the identity term only contributes a constant shift in energy and may be ignored. observe that we have reduced the double-factorized hamiltonian to sums of products of basis-rotated majorana operators  $\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma ,x}$ .

we thus block-encode the two-body term as follows.

1. block-encode  $\mathcal{b}[\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}]$  of the basis transformed majorana operator section vii c 1.  
2. block-encode  $\mathcal{b}[\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}]$  by multiplying  $\mathcal{b}[\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}]$  section vii c2.  
3. block-encode  $\mathcal{b}\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]$  by taking a linear combination of  $\lambda_k\mathcal{b}\left[\gamma_{\vec{r}_k,\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{r}_k,\sigma,1}\right]$  over the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of  $l$  and the spins section vii c 3.  
4. block-encode  $\mathcal{b}\left[t_2\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]\right]$  by applying  $\mathcal{b}\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]$  twice using qubitization [33].  
5. block-encode  $\mathcal{b}\left[\frac{\mathrm{two}_h}{\frac{1}{4}\sum_r\|l^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{sc}}^2}\right]$  by taking a linear combination of  $\| l^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{sc}}^2\mathcal{b}\left[t_2\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_{l^{(r)}}}{\|l^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]\right]$  over the rank components of the two-electron tensor section vii c 4.  
6. block-encode  $\mathcal{b}\left[\frac{h_{\mathrm{df}}}{\|l^{(-1)}\|_{\mathrm{sc}} + \frac{1}{4}\sum_r\|l^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{sc}}^2}\right]$  by adding one- and two-body terms sec-tion vii c5.

generally, the cost of block-encoding the large number of two-electron terms dominate that of the smaller number of one-electron terms. a common theme throughout will be the use of symmetries. many coefficients turn out to be identical. for instance,  $l_{ij}^{(r)} = l_{ji}^{(r)}$ , and is independent of spin. moreover, the same coefficients  $\vec{r}_k^{(r)}$  occur in both of  $\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}$ . wherever possible, we use this redundancy to optimize the number of bits of classical data we need to encode into our quantum circuits. these optimizations are combined with recent advances using ancillary qubits [31] to substantially reduce toffoli gate count

we now provide optimized quantum circuits that implement the above steps. we make heavy use of quantum circuit notation, outlined in section vii a.

# 1. block-encoded, basis-transformed majorana operator

we synthesize the basis-transformed majorana operator  $\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}$  by conjugating  $\gamma_{0,\sigma,x}$  with a sequence of unitary rotations. the required sequence of unitary rotations follows from the following observation.

lemma 8 (sum of majorana operators by majorana rotations). let the unitary  $u_{\vec{u}}$  be the sequence

$$
u _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , x} \doteq v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , x} ^ {(0)} v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , x} ^ {(1)} \dots v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , x} ^ {(n - 2)}, \quad v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , x} ^ {(p)} \doteq e ^ {\theta_ {p} \gamma_ {p, \sigma , x} \gamma_ {p + 1, \sigma , x}}. \tag {55}
$$

then for all  $\sigma \in \{0,1\}$  and  $x \in \{0,1\}$ , there exists rotation angles  $\theta_p \doteq \theta_{\vec{u},p}$  that are functions of  $\vec{u}$  such that  $u_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}^{\dagger} \cdot \gamma_{0,\sigma,x} \cdot u_{\vec{u},\sigma,x} = \gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}$ .

proof. in the interests of clarity, we drop the  $\sigma, x$  subscript in the following. by taking a taylor series expansion, observe that  $v_{\vec{u}}^{(p)} = \cos (\theta_p)\mathcal{i} + \sin (\theta_p)\gamma_p\gamma_{p+1}$ . thus

$$
v _ {\vec {u}} ^ {(p) \dagger} \gamma_ {q} v _ {\vec {u}} ^ {(p)} = \left\{ \begin{array}{l l} \gamma_ {q}, & q \neq p, p + 1, \\ \cos \left(2 \theta_ {p}\right) \gamma_ {p} + \sin \left(2 \theta_ {p}\right) \gamma_ {p + 1}, & q = p, \\ \cos \left(2 \theta_ {p}\right) \gamma_ {p + 1} - \sin \left(2 \theta_ {p}\right) \gamma_ {p}, & q = p + 1. \end{array} \right. \tag {56}
$$

thus  $u_{\vec{u}}^{\dagger}\cdot \gamma_{0}\cdot u_{\vec{u}} = \sum_{p\in [n]}u_{p}\gamma_{p}$ , by choosing

$$
u _ {0} = \cos \left(2 \theta_ {0}\right), \quad u _ {1} = \sin \left(2 \theta_ {0}\right) \cos \left(2 \theta_ {1}\right), \quad \dots , u _ {p} = \cos \left(2 \theta_ {p}\right) \prod_ {j <   p} \sin \left(2 \theta_ {j}\right). \tag {57}
$$

we obtain the angles  $\theta_{p}$  by recursively solving this linear chain of equations.

![](images/5c9dadef509a2b1c14e72256e81acb0fd74796050c4b459db71aa0f6091099be.jpg)

as  $\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}$  is unitary, it is also trivially its own block-encoding  $\mathcal{b}[\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}] = \gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,x}$ .

# 2. block-encoded product of majorana operators

we synthesize the product  $\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}$  using the implementation described in lemma 8. observe that the commutator  $[\gamma_{p,\sigma,x}\gamma_{p+1,\sigma,x},\gamma_{q,\rho,y}\gamma_{q+1,\rho,y}]$  is zero for all  $\sigma \neq \rho$  or  $x \neq y$ . thus all rotations in  $u_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}$  and  $u_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}$  commute with each other. this allows us to collect rotations in the product

$$
\gamma_ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 1} = \left(\left(v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 0} ^ {(0)} v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 1} ^ {(0)}\right) \dots \left(v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 0} ^ {(n - 2)} v _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 1} ^ {(n - 2)}\right)\right) ^ {\dagger} \gamma_ {0, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {0, \sigma , 1} \underbrace {\left(v _ {\vec {u} , \sigma , 0} ^ {(0)} v _ {\vec {u} , \sigma , 1} ^ {(0)}\right) \cdots \left(v _ {\vec {u} , \sigma , 0} ^ {(n - 2)} v _ {\vec {u} , \sigma , 1} ^ {(n - 2)}\right)} _ {u _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 0} u _ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 1}}. \tag {58}
$$

in a pauli representation of the majorana operators, each rotation

$$
v _ {\vec {u}, p, x} = c _ {p, x} ^ {\dagger} \cdot e ^ {i \theta_ {\vec {u}, p} z} \cdot c _ {p, x} \tag {59}
$$

is implemented by a single-qubit  $z$  rotation conjugated by some clifford gate  $c_{p,x}$  such that  $c_{p,x}^{\dagger} \cdot iz \cdot c_{p,x} = \gamma_{p,\sigma,x}\gamma_{p+1,\sigma,x}$ . we use the jordan-wigner representation (momentarily ignoring the spin index) that maps

$$
\gamma_ {p, 0} \gamma_ {p + 1, 0} \rightarrow - i y _ {p} x _ {p + 1}, \quad \gamma_ {p, 1} \gamma_ {p + 1, 1} \rightarrow i x _ {p} y _ {p + 1}, \quad \gamma_ {0, 0} \gamma_ {0, 1} \rightarrow i z _ {0}. \tag {60}
$$

in the example where  $n = 4$ , the rotated majorana operator is hence implemented by the circuit

$$
= \begin{array}{c c c} \hline \gamma \vec {u}, 0 \gamma \vec {u}, 1 \\ \hline \end{array} \quad = \begin{array}{c c c} \hline u _ {\vec {u}, 0} u _ {\vec {u}, 1} \\ \hline \end{array} \quad \begin{array}{c c c} \hline i z \\ \hline \end{array} \quad \begin{array}{c c c} \hline (u _ {\vec {u}, 0} u _ {\vec {u}, 1}) ^ {\dagger} \\ \hline \end{array} , \tag {61}
$$

where the basis transformation is

$$
\vdots \begin{array}{c} \boxed {+} \\ \left(\overline {{u _ {\vec {u} , 0} u _ {\vec {u} , 1}}}\right) \\ = \frac {\boxed {e ^ {- i \theta_ {0} x _ {0} y _ {1}}} \boxed {e ^ {i \theta_ {0} y _ {0} x _ {1}}}}{\boxed {e ^ {- i \theta_ {1} x _ {1} y _ {2}}} \boxed {e ^ {i \theta_ {1} y _ {1} x _ {2}}}} \dots -, \end{array} \tag {62}
$$

which, upon substituting the jordan-wigner representation and defining the  $z$  pauli rotation  $r_{\theta} \doteq e^{i\theta z}$ , is equal to

$$
\left(\overline {{u _ {i , 0} u _ {i , 1}}}\right) ^ {\dagger} = \frac {\left[ \begin{array}{l l l l l l l l l l l l l l l l} c _ {0 , 0} & & & & & & & & & & & & & & \\ & r _ {\theta_ {0}} ^ {\dagger} & & & & & & & & & & & & & \\ & & c _ {0 , 0} ^ {\dagger} & & & & & & & & & & & & \\ & & c _ {0 , 1} ^ {\dagger} & & & & & & & & & & & & \\ & & r _ {\theta_ {0}} ^ {\dagger} & & & & & & & & & & & \\ & & c _ {0 , 1} ^ {\dagger} & & & & & & \\ & & c _ {1 , 0} ^ {\dagger} & & r _ {\theta_ {1}} ^ {\dagger} & & c _ {1 , 0} ^ {\dagger} & & c _ {1 , 1} ^ {\dagger} & & r _ {\theta_ {1}} ^ {\dagger} & & c _ {1 , 1} ^ {\dagger} \end{array} \right] . \quad \dots \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad . \quad .
$$

with the spin indices restored, the jordan-wigner representation maps

$$
\gamma_ {p, 1, 0} \gamma_ {p + 1, 1, 0} \rightarrow - i y _ {p + n} x _ {p + 1 + n}, \quad \gamma_ {p, 1, 1} \gamma_ {p + 1, 1, 1} \rightarrow i x _ {p + n} y _ {p + 1 + n}, \quad \gamma_ {0, 1, 0} \gamma_ {0, 1, 1} \rightarrow i z _ {n}, \tag {64}
$$

for  $\sigma = 1$ . the case  $\sigma = 0$  is identical to eq. (60). thus the quantum circuit for

$$
\gamma_ {\vec {u}, 1, 0} \gamma_ {\vec {u}, 1, 1} = \prod_ {j = 0} ^ {n - 1} \left(\mathrm {s w a p} _ {j \leftrightarrow j + n}\right) \cdot \gamma_ {\vec {u}, 0} \gamma_ {\vec {u}, 1} \prod_ {j = 0} ^ {n - 1} \cdot \left(\mathrm {s w a p} _ {j \leftrightarrow j + n}\right), \tag {65}
$$

is implemented by swapping all pairs of qubits  $j \leftrightarrow j + n$ . thus the spin-multiplexed unitary  $|0\rangle \langle 0| \otimes \gamma_{\vec{u},0,0} \gamma_{\vec{u},0,1} + |1\rangle \langle 1| \otimes \gamma_{\vec{u},1,0} \gamma_{\vec{u},1,1}$  is implemented by adding a control to the swap gates, as illustrated below (for  $n = 4$ ).

$$
\begin{array}{l} \overbrace {\gamma u _ {i} \sigma_ {i} 0 ^ {j} \gamma u _ {i} \sigma_ {i}} ^ {\sigma} \\ = \end{array} \left[ \begin{array}{c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c} \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & \bullet & 1 \\ * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * \\ * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * \\ * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * \\ * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * &* \\ * & * & * & * & * & * & * & * \\
$$

as  $\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}$  is unitary, it is also trivially its own block-encoding  $\mathcal{b}[\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}] = \gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}$ .

# 3. block-encoded one-electron operator and its square

the one-electron operator is

$$
\mathrm {o n e} _ {l} \doteq \frac {1}{2} \sum_ {k \in [ k ]} \lambda_ {k} \sum_ {\sigma} \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 1}, \quad l = \sum_ {k \in [ k ]} \lambda_ {k} \vec {r} _ {k} \vec {r} _ {k} ^ {\dagger}. \tag {67}
$$

the block-encoding of  $\mathrm{one}_l$  is accomplished by adding block-encodings of  $\mathcal{b}[\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,0}\gamma_{\vec{u},\sigma,1}]$ , each weighted by the eigenvalue  $\lambda_k$ , following the construction in eq. (13). this requires synthesizing the quantum state

$$
| \vec {\lambda} \rangle = \sum_ {k} \sqrt {| \lambda_ {k} | / \| l \| _ {\mathrm {s c}}} | k \rangle | \operatorname {s i g n} [ \lambda_ {k} ] \rangle \tag {68}
$$

and the multiplexed unitary

$$
z \otimes \sum_ {k} \sum_ {\sigma} | k \rangle \langle k | \otimes | \sigma \rangle \langle \sigma | \otimes \mathcal {b} \left[ \gamma_ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {\vec {u}, \sigma , 1} \right]. \tag {69}
$$

note that we encode the sign of  $\lambda_{k}$  in a single qubit using the pauli  $z$  operation, as described in eq. (29), which uses the fact  $\langle \mathrm{sign}[\lambda_k]|z|\mathrm{sign}[\lambda_k]\rangle = \mathrm{sign}[\lambda_k]$ . in the following circuit diagrams, this sign qubit is assumed to be implicitly present and will not be shown. given these two components, the block-encoding is implemented by the quantum circuit

$$
\left[ \begin{array}{c} \mathcal {b} \left[ \frac {\operatorname {o n e} _ {l}}{\| l \| _ {s c}} \right] \\ \hline \end{array} \right] = \begin{array}{c} - \boxed {| \vec {\lambda} \rangle} \\ \hline - \boxed {\mathrm {h a d}} \\ \hline \end{array} \begin{array}{c} k \\ \hline \sigma \\ \hline \end{array} \begin{array}{c} | \vec {\lambda} \rangle^ {\dagger} \\ \hline \mathrm {h a d} \\ \hline \end{array}. \tag {70}
$$

we now discuss the implementation of eq. (69) which is controlled by the spin index  $\sigma$  and the eigenvalue index  $k$ . control by the spin index remains unchanged from eq. (66). control by the eigenvalue index is implemented by multiplexing the basis transformation

$$
\sum_ {k} | k \rangle \langle k | \otimes u _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, 0} u _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, 1}. \tag {71}
$$

from eq. (63),  $u_{\vec{r}_k,0}u_{\vec{r}_k,1}$  is a sequence of  $z$ -rotations by a  $k$ -dependent angle  $\theta_{\vec{r}_k,p}$ , each conjugated by a clifford gate that is independent of  $k$ . thus eq. (71) may be implemented by multiplexing the phase-rotations

$$
\sum_ {k} | k \rangle \langle k | \otimes e ^ {i \theta \bar {r} _ {k, p} z}, \tag {72}
$$

as follows

![](images/37ac2260b6836d3e78ef78c4146758d12c6b3a9ed072de85cbff7dbef070f54e.jpg)

we implement multiplexed phase-rotations using a data-lookup oracle  $d_{p}|k\rangle |z\rangle = |k\rangle |z\oplus \tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p}\rangle$  on  $k$ $\beta$ -bit entries. this oracle computes a  $\beta$ -bit binary representation of the rotation angle  $\frac{\theta_{\vec{r}_k,p}}{2\pi} \approx \tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p} = \sum_{b = 0}^{\beta -1}\tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p,b} / 2^{1 + b} \in [0,1 - 2^{-\beta}]$ , and may be implemented according to section vii a 1. with this oracle, we perform the following sequence

$$
d _ {p} | k \rangle | 0 \rangle | \psi \rangle = | k \rangle | \tilde {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p} \rangle | \psi \rangle = | k \rangle \left(\bigotimes_ {b = 0} ^ {\beta - 1} | \tilde {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p, b} \rangle\right) | \psi \rangle \tag {74}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {c o n t r o l l e d r o t a t i o n s} \to | k \rangle \left(\bigotimes_ {b = 0} ^ {\beta - 1} | \tilde {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p, b} \rangle\right) \prod_ {b = 0} ^ {\beta - 1} e ^ {i 2 \pi \tilde {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p, b} z} | \psi \rangle = | k \rangle | \tilde {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p, b} \rangle e ^ {i 2 \pi \tilde {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p} z} | \psi \rangle \\ \mathrm {u n c o m p u t e} \rightarrow | k \rangle | 0 \rangle e ^ {i 2 \pi \bar {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p} z} | \psi \rangle . \\ \end{array}
$$

this costs two queries to data-lookup  $d_{p}$  and  $\beta$  arbitrary single-qubit rotations. as the circuit for eq. (69) contains  $4n$  multiplexed rotations, these rotations cost at most  $8n$  queries to data-lookup on  $k$ $\beta$ -bit entries, and  $4n\beta$  arbitrary single-qubit rotations.

however, some optimizations are possible. first, the rotation angle bits computed once by  $d_{p}$  may be used to implement both rotations  $r_{\tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p}}$  in eq. (73). this reduces queries by a factor of two to  $4n$ . second, the uncomputation of  $|\tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p}\rangle$  may be merged with the computation of  $\tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p+1}$ . instead of applying  $d_{p}$  and  $d_{p+1}$ , we merge them into a single data-lookup oracle  $d_{p,p+1}|k\rangle|z\rangle = |p\rangle|z \oplus \tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p} \oplus \tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p+1}\rangle$ . this reduces queries by another factor of two to  $2n$ . third, the data-lookup oracles may output  $\kappa > \beta$  bits. thus multiple angles may be written out at once, e.g.  $|z_0 \oplus \tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p}\rangle|z_1 \oplus \tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p+1}\rangle \cdots |z_{\kappa/\beta-1} \oplus \tilde{\theta}_{\vec{r}_k,p+\kappa/\beta-1}\rangle$ . these angles allow the application of more controlled-rotations for each application of data-lookup. this is detailed in section vii b 1, and reduces the number of queries to  $2\lceil \frac{n\beta}{\kappa} \rceil$ , but now to data-lookup on  $k$ $\kappa$ -bit entries. fourth, many of arbitrary single-qubit rotations are by identical angles (powers of 2). these may be implemented exactly using  $4n\beta$  toffoli gates by the phase gradient technique [34]. this also requires a one-time cost of preparing and storing  $\beta$  single-qubit resource states whose cost is negligible and thus ignored. fifth, using the choice  $\kappa = n\beta$ , only one computation and uncomputation step is required. this can be advantageous as uncomputation as described in section vii a 1 has a toffoli cost that can be smaller than computation when many ancillary qubits are used.

we now bound the bits of precision  $\beta$  required to approximate eq. (69) to an overall error of  $\epsilon$  in spectral norm. as each rotation angle  $\theta_{\vec{r}_k,p}$  is approximated to an error of at most  $\pi 2^{-\beta}$ , the error of each unitary rotation compared to its binary approximation is

$$
\left\| r _ {\theta_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p}} - r _ {2 \pi \tilde {\theta} _ {\vec {r} _ {k}, p}} \right\| \leq \left\| r _ {\pi 2 ^ {- \beta}} - \mathcal {i} \right\| = \left\| \cos \left(2 ^ {- \beta} \pi\right) - i \sin \left(2 ^ {- \beta} \pi\right) z - \mathcal {i} \right\| \tag {75}
$$

the cumulative error  $\epsilon \leq (4n)\pi 2^{-\beta +1 / 2}$  of all rotations then follows by the triangle inequality on unitary operators  $\| (\prod_ju_j) - (\prod_j\tilde{u}_j)\| \leq \sum_j\| u_j - \tilde{u}_j\|$ . thus the bits of precision required is

$$
\beta = \left\lceil \frac {1}{2} + \log_ {2} \left(\frac {4 n \pi}{\epsilon}\right)\right\rceil . \tag {76}
$$

including the error  $\mu$  of state-preparation results in an  $\epsilon' = \epsilon + \mu$ -approximate block-encoding  $\mathcal{b}_{\epsilon + \mu}\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]$  instead of the exact  $\mathcal{b}\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]$ . a simple choice of error budgeting is  $\epsilon = \epsilon'/2$

table xxiv. resources used to block-encode a one-electron operator  $\mathcal{b}_{\epsilon}\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]$  on  $n$  orbitals where  $l$  has  $k$  eigenvectors. the parameters  $\beta = \left[5.152 + \log_2\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)\right]$  and  $\mu = 2 + \lceil \log_2(1 / \epsilon)\rceil$

<table><tr><td>operation</td><td>#</td><td>toffoli cost each</td><td>ancillary qubits</td></tr><tr><td>data-lookup lemma 2</td><td>1</td><td>dk,nβ,λ + duk,nβ,λ</td><td>2[log2k] + nβ(1 + λ)</td></tr><tr><td>arbitrary rotations [34]</td><td>4nβ</td><td>1</td><td>0</td></tr><tr><td>controlled-swaps</td><td>2n</td><td>1</td><td>0</td></tr><tr><td>state-preparation lemma 4</td><td>2</td><td>μ + dk,nk+μ+1,nβ(1+λ)+2n+1 dirty</td><td>[log2k] + 2μ + 1</td></tr></table>

and  $\mu = \epsilon' / 2$ , though this may be optimized with more weight on  $\epsilon$  as its data-lookup oracles are more costly.

the overall cost of block-encoding the one-electron operator is summarized in table xxiv, and is dominated by data-lookup in multiplexing the basis transformation rotations. as state-preparation is cheap in comparison, we choose an implementation that is assisted by  $\kappa + \lambda + 2n$  dirty qubits. given  $\mathcal{b}_{\epsilon}\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]$ , qubitization in theorem 1 describes how it may be applied twice, hence doubling the cost, to block-encode  $\mathcal{b}_{2\epsilon}\left[t_2\left[\frac{\mathrm{one}_l}{\|l\|_{\mathrm{sc}}}\right]\right]$ . this also applies a reflection about the all-zero state  $|0\dots 0\rangle$  on  $\mathcal{o}(\log_k + \mu)$  qubits, whose cost is negligible and thus ignored.

# 4. block-encoded two-electron operator

in this section, we describe a block-encoding of the double-factorized hamiltonian in eq. (54). we focus on the two-body term, which is the most costly component.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {t w o} _ {h} \doteq \frac {1}{4} \sum_ {r \in [ r ]} \| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2} t _ {2} \left[ \frac {\mathrm {o n e} _ {l ^ {(r)}}}{\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}}} \right], \quad \overrightarrow {\lambda_ {\mathrm {s h}}} \doteq (\| l ^ {(1)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2}, \| l ^ {(2)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2}, \dots , \| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2}) ^ {\top}, (7 7) \\ l ^ {(r)} = \sum_ {k \in [ m ^ {(r)} ]} \lambda_ {k} ^ {(r)} \vec {r} _ {k} ^ {(r)} \vec {r} _ {k} ^ {(r) \top}. \\ \end{array}
$$

at a high level, we achieve this block-encoding using the following circuit identities, which are defined in section vii a.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \text {a d d b l o c k - e n c o d i n g s e q .} (1 3): \boxed {\mathcal {b} \left[ \frac {\mathrm {t w o} _ {h}}{\frac {1}{4} \| \overrightarrow {\lambda_ {\mathrm {s h}}} \| _ {1}} \right] = \frac {- \sqrt {| \overrightarrow {\lambda_ {s h}} \rangle}}{\sqrt {\mathcal {b} \left[ t _ {2} \left[ \frac {\operatorname {o n e} _ {l} (r)}{\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {s c}} \right] \right]}} \frac {\langle r \rangle}{\langle \lambda_ {s h} \rangle^ {\dagger}},} (78) \\ \text {q u b i t i z a t i o n} (79) \\ \end{array}
$$

$$
\text {a d d b l o c k - e n c o d i n g s e q .} (1 3): \quad \left. \begin{array}{c} \boxed {r} \\ \mathcal {b} \left[ \frac {\operatorname {o n e} _ {l ^ {(r)}}}{\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {s c}} \right] \\ \end{array} \right\} = \begin{array}{c} \boxed {r} \\ \boxed {| \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle} \\ \boxed {h a d} \\ \boxed {\gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 1}} \end{array} \boxed {r} \begin{array}{c} \vec {r} \\ k \\ | \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle^ {\dagger} \\ h a d \end{array} \tag {80}
$$

compared to the one-electron operator in eq. (70), we see that the only new element is multiplexing by another control variable  $r$ . thus instead of the singly-multiplexed basis transformation in eq. (73), we implement the doubly-multiplexed basis transform

$$
\therefore \left[ \begin{array}{l} \frac {r}{k} \\ \left(u \dot {r} _ {k , 0} u \dot {r} _ {k , 1}\right) ^ {\dagger} \\ \vdots \end{array} \right] = \begin{array}{c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c c} \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \frac {r}{k} & \dots \\ c _ {0, 0} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 0}}} ^ {+} & c _ {0, 0} ^ {+} & c _ {0, 1} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 0}}} ^ {+} & c _ {0, 1} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 0} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 1}}} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 0} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 1}}} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 1} ^ {+} & \dots \\ & & & & c _ {1, 0} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 1}}} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 0} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 1} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 1}}} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 1} ^ {+} & \dots \\ & & & & c _ {1, 0} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 2}}} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 0} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 1} ^ {+} & r _ {\theta_ {r _ {k , 2}}} ^ {+} & c _ {1, 1} ^ {+} & \dots \\ \vdots \end{array} . \tag {81}
$$

following the implementation of multiplexed-phase rotations in eq. (74), eq. (81) can be implemented given a multiplexed data-lookup oracle  $dd_{p}|r\rangle |k\rangle |z\rangle = |r\rangle |k\rangle |z\oplus \theta_{\vec{r}_{k}^{(r)},p}\rangle$

two challenges make the implementation of  $dd_{p}$  non-obvious. first, the number of entries  $k \in [m^{(r)}]$  can depend on the index  $r$ . rather than storing the worst-case number of  $r\max_{r}m^{(r)}$  entries, storing only  $\sum_{r \in [r]} m^{(r)}$  entries would minimize the cost of data-lookup. second, the multiplexed data-lookup oracle should allow for the best possible trade-off between toffoli gates and assisting ancillary qubits. our implementation of  $dd_{p}$  is detailed in section vii b 2.

the next challenge is multiplexed state preparation of  $|\vec{\lambda}^{(r)}\rangle$ . by drawing eq. (78) in full and inserting identity in the middle,

$$
\begin{array}{c} \overrightarrow {| \lambda_ {s h} \rangle} \\ \overrightarrow {| \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle} \\ \overrightarrow {h a d} \\ \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 0} \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 1} \end{array} \begin{array}{c} r \\ k \\ \sigma \\ \text {h a d} \end{array} \begin{array}{c} r \\ | \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle^ {\dagger} \\ \text {h a d} \end{array} \begin{array}{c} r \\ | \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle \\ \text {h a d} \end{array} \begin{array}{c} r \\ | \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle^ {\dagger} \\ \sigma \\ \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 1} \gamma_ {\vec {r} _ {k}, \sigma , 0} \end{array}
$$

table xxv. resources used to block-encode a two-electron operator  $\mathcal{b}_{\epsilon}\left[\frac{\mathrm{two}_h}{\frac{1}{4}\sum_r\|l^{(r)}\|_{\mathrm{sc}}^2}\right]$  on  $n$  orbitals where  $m$  is the number of eigenvectors in the doubly-factorized representation. above,  $\beta = \left\lceil 5.652 + \log_2\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)\right\rceil$ ,  $\mu = \lceil 2.5 + \log_2(1 / \epsilon)\rceil$ , and  $c = \mathrm{d}_{m,2n_{\max_r m(r)} + 2n_r + \mu +1,\lambda +\kappa +2n}$  dirty

<table><tr><td>operation</td><td>applications</td><td>toffoli cost each</td><td>ancillary qubits</td></tr><tr><td>data-lookup lemma 7</td><td>2</td><td>dm,r,nβ,λ + dum,r,nβ,λ max [log2m], [log2r] + λnβ</td><td rowspan="2">0</td></tr><tr><td>arbitrary rotations [34]</td><td>8nβ</td><td>1</td></tr><tr><td>controlled-swaps</td><td>4n</td><td>1</td><td>0</td></tr><tr><td>state-preparation lemma 5</td><td>4</td><td>μ + c</td><td>nk + 2μ + 1</td></tr></table>

this suggests preparing the quantum state represented by

$$
\begin{array}{l} \begin{array}{l} | 0 \rangle \xrightarrow {\boxed {|\overrightarrow {\lambda_ {s h}} \rangle}} \boxed {r} \\ | 0 \rangle \xrightarrow {\boxed {| \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle}} = \sum_ {r \in [ r ]} \sqrt {\frac {\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2}}{\| \overrightarrow {\lambda_ {\mathrm {s h}}} \| _ {1}}} | r \rangle | \vec {\lambda} ^ {(r)} \rangle = \sum_ {r \in [ r ]} \sqrt {\frac {\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} ^ {2}}{\| \overrightarrow {\lambda_ {\mathrm {s h}}} \| _ {1}}} | r \rangle \sum_ {k \in [ m ^ {(r)} ]} \sqrt {\frac {\lambda_ {k} ^ {(r)}}{\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}}}} | k \rangle \end{array} \\ = \sum_ {r \in [ r ]} \sum_ {k \in [ m ^ {(r)} ]} \sqrt {\frac {\| l ^ {(r)} \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} \lambda_ {k} ^ {(r)}}{\| \overrightarrow {\lambda_ {\mathrm {s h}}} \| _ {1}}} | r \rangle | k \rangle . \tag {83} \\ \end{array}
$$

however, it is difficult to apply the reflection in this case.

thus we use the multiplexed sparse data-lookup oracle of lemma 7 in the state preparation routine of lemma 5. in this case, the desired state with  $m = \sum_{r\in [r]}m^{(r)}$  coefficients can be created with a cost that scales with that outlined in lemma 5.

the overall cost of block-encoding the two-electron operator is summarized in table xxv, and is dominated by data-lookup in multiplexing the basis transformation rotations.

# 5. asymptotic costs

although toffoli gate costs may be obtained by summing over elements in table xxv, it is informative to evaluate asymptotic costs for large  $n$ . note that empirical fits by peng et al. [27] observe that  $r = \mathcal{o}(n)$  and  $m^{(r)} = \mathcal{o}(\log n)$  when  $n \gg 1000$ , in the case where  $n$  scales with the number of atoms in the systems considered. in our main text, we vary the active space  $n \leq 250$  for systems with a fixed number of atoms and observe a more conservative scaling of  $r = \mathcal{o}(n^{1.5})$ ,  $m^{(r)} = \mathcal{o}(n)$ , and hence  $m = \mathcal{o}(n^{2.5})$ . we also choose the number of qubits that store the fermionic basis transform angles to be  $\kappa = n\beta$ , which is just a constant factor more than the  $2n$  qubits representing spin-orbitals, as  $\beta = \left\lceil 5.652 + \log_2\left(\frac{n}{\epsilon}\right)\right\rceil$ .

thus the computation of data-lookup has a cost

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {d} _ {m, r, n \beta , \lambda} \leq 2 \mathrm {d} _ {r, n _ {m}, n _ {m} + n \beta + \lambda + 2 n \text {d i r t y}} + \mathrm {d} _ {m, n \beta , \lambda} + 2 n _ {m} + \mathcal {o} (1) \tag {84} \\ = \mathrm {d} _ {m, n \beta , \lambda} + \mathcal {o} (\sqrt {r n _ {m}}) \\ = \min  _ {\lambda^ {\prime} \in [ 0, \lambda ]} \left(\frac {m}{1 + \lambda^ {\prime}} + n \beta \lambda^ {\prime}\right) + \mathcal {o} (\sqrt {r n _ {m}}), \\ \end{array}
$$

and its uncomputation has a cost

$$
\mathrm {d u} _ {m, r, n \beta , \lambda^ {\prime \prime}} = \min  _ {\lambda^ {\prime} \in [ 0, \lambda^ {\prime \prime} ]} \left(\frac {m}{1 + \lambda^ {\prime}} + \lambda^ {\prime}\right) + \mathcal {o} (\sqrt {r n _ {m}}). \tag {85}
$$

as the number of clean ancilla qubits used by the computation step is  $\sim \lambda' n\beta$ , the uncomputation step may choose  $\lambda'' = \lambda' n\beta$ . with this choice, the toffoli cost of  $\mathrm{du}_{m,r,n\beta,\lambda''}$  is always a factor of at least  $\sqrt{n\beta}$  smaller than  $\mathrm{d}_{m,r,n\beta,\lambda}$ , so long as  $m = \omega(n\beta)$ , and is sub-dominant. for instance, in the case of  $\lambda = 0$ , we obtain almost-linear scaling with respect to  $m$  as follows.

$$
\mathrm {d} _ {m, r, n \beta , 0} = m + \mathcal {o} (\sqrt {r n _ {m}}) = \mathcal {o} (n \log n). \tag {86}
$$

choosing  $\lambda = \theta (\sqrt{m / (n\beta)})$  minimizes this as  $\mathrm{d}_{m,r,n\beta ,\theta (\sqrt{m / (n\beta)})} = \mathcal{o}(\sqrt{mn\beta}) + \mathcal{o}(\sqrt{rn_m})$

state-preparation has a cost that is sub-dominant to data-lookup. it suffices to use  $\mu = \lceil 2.5 + \log_2(1 / \epsilon)\rceil$  bits of precision.

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {s t a t e} _ {m, 2 n _ {r} + \mu + 1,} = \mu + \mathrm {d} _ {m, b, \lambda + \kappa + 2 n \text {d i r t y}} \tag {87} \\ = \min _ {\lambda^ {\prime} \in [ 0, \lambda + n (2 + \beta) ]} \left(2 \frac {m}{1 + \lfloor \frac {\lambda^ {\prime}}{b} \rfloor} + 4 b \left\lfloor \frac {\lambda^ {\prime}}{b} \right\rfloor\right) + \mu , \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $b = 2n_{\max_{r \in [r]} m^{(r)}} + 2n_r + \mu + 1 = \mathcal{o}(\log n + \mu)$ . as the number of dirty qubits  $n(2 + \beta) \gg b$ ,

$$
\operatorname {s t a t e} _ {m, 2 n _ {r} + \mu + 1,} \lesssim \min  \left[ m, 4 \sqrt {2 m b} \right] + \mu = \mathcal {o} (\sqrt {m \log (1 / \epsilon)}). \tag {88}
$$

thus the total number of toffoli gates is

$$
\begin{array}{l} 2 \left(\mathrm {d} _ {m, r, n \beta , \lambda} + \mathrm {d u} _ {m, r, n \beta , \lambda}\right) + 4 \left(\text {s t a t e} _ {m, 2 n _ {r} + \mu + 1}, + n (2 \beta + 1)\right) \tag {89} \\ \leq \min _ {\lambda^ {\prime} \in [ 0, \lambda ]} \left(\frac {2 m}{1 + \lambda^ {\prime}} + 2 \lambda^ {\prime} n \beta + 4 n (2 \beta + 1)\right) + \mathcal {o} (\sqrt {r n _ {m}} + \sqrt {m \log (1 / \epsilon)}). \\ \end{array}
$$

note that converting this block-encoding to a quantum walk using the qubitization procedure theorem 1 requires an additional reflection ref about the state  $|0\cdots 0\rangle$  on the ancillary register of the block-encoding. this reflection may be implemented using a number of toffoli gates that is at most equal to the number of qubits comprising the ancillary register. importantly, this ancillary register excludes any additional clean or dirty qubits that are used in intermediate steps, such as in data-lookup. thus the cost of ref is subdominant to that of the block-encoding.

# viii. matrix schatten and entrywise 1-norm

let  $h \in \mathbb{c}^{n \times n}$  be any hermitian matrix. this matrix has eigenvalues  $h \cdot u_k = \sigma_k u_k$ , where the orthonormal  $k^{\text{th}}$  eigenvector  $u_k$  can be viewed as the  $k^{\text{th}}$  column of some unitary matrix  $u \in \mathbb{c}^{n \times n}$ . we now prove that the following inequality is true

$$
\frac {1}{n} \| h \| _ {\mathrm {e w}} \leq \| h \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} \leq \| h \| _ {\mathrm {e w}}, \tag {90}
$$

where

$$
\| h \| _ {\mathrm {s c}} \doteq \sum_ {k \in [ n ]} | \sigma_ {k} |, \quad \| h \| _ {\mathrm {e w}} \doteq \sum_ {j, k \in [ n ]} | h _ {j k} |. \tag {91}
$$

moreover, we show that this inequality is tight. we have not been able to find a proof of this statement in the literature, so our derivation may be of independent interest.

to prove the lower bound, observe that

$$
\sum_ {k \in [ n ]} | \sigma_ {k} | \geq \sqrt {\sum_ {k \in [ n ]} | \sigma_ {k} | ^ {2}} = \sqrt {\sum_ {j , k \in [ n ]} | h _ {j k} | ^ {2}} \geq \frac {1}{n} \sum_ {j, k \in [ n ]} | h _ {j k} |. \tag {92}
$$

the middle equality follows from the definition of the frobenius norm, and the first and last inequalities follow from the usual vector norm inequality  $\sqrt{\sum_{j\in[m]}|x_j|^2}\leq \sum_{j\in [m]}|x_j|\leq \sqrt{m\sum_{j\in[m]}|x_j|^2}$  for any vector  $x$ . this is an equality when every entry in  $h$  is one, and is hence tight.

to prove the upper bound, observe that

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sum_ {k \in [ n ]} | \sigma_ {k} | = \sum_ {k \in [ n ]} | (u _ {k}) ^ {\dagger} \cdot h \cdot u _ {k} | = \sum_ {k \in [ n ]} \left| \sum_ {i, j \in [ n ]} u _ {k i} ^ {\dagger} h _ {i j} u _ {j k} \right| \leq \sum_ {i, j \in [ n ]} \left(\sum_ {k \in [ n ]} \left| u _ {k i} ^ {\dagger} \right| | u _ {j k} |\right) | h _ {i j} | \tag {93} \\ \leq \sum_ {i, j \in [ n ]} | h _ {i j} |. \\ \end{array}
$$

the final inequality follows from applying the cauchy-schwartz inequality on the term in round brackets. using the fact that any row or column of any unitary has unit 2-norm,

$$
\sum_ {k \in [ n ]} \left| u _ {k i} ^ {\dagger} \right| | u _ {j k} | \leq \sqrt {\sum_ {k \in [ n ]} \left| u _ {k i} ^ {\dagger} \right| ^ {2}} \sqrt {\sum_ {k \in [ n ]} \left| u _ {j k} \right| ^ {2}} = 1. \tag {94}
$$

this upper bound is also an equality by choosing  $h$  to contain only a single non-zero entry that lies on the diagonal, and is hence tight.
